Do not duplicate `symfony/http-foundation` in Cookie's package manifest: Cookie's direct structural dependency on Hypervel HTTP already supplies it, and the repository deliberately does not mirror every imported transitive HTTP type into every package manifest.
- **Cross-package implications:** Contracts owns the truthful QueueingFactory types; Support owns the Cookie facade; Boost owns the response documentation. The completed `support-02` enum boundary is revalidated at the Cookie facade without changing value-oriented helpers or unrelated response/helper APIs.
- **Regression strategy:** Cover facade access through unit, string-backed, integer-backed, and zero-backed enum names; mixed facade defaults; nested request cookie arrays through the jar and contract; non-Cookie queued defaults; queued-cookie element metadata; and the existing selective-encryption precedence behavior. Run changed tests immediately, then `composer fix`, stale-reference scans, and a fresh caller/callee and lifecycle review.
- **Performance and compatibility:** Ordinary string calls keep their existing path apart from the facade's required local fallback expression. Enum calls perform the already-established conversion, and all other changes are type metadata, tests, or documentation. No lock, allocation class, service resolution, context slot, cache, retained worker state, or new abstraction is added. Laravel-facing call shapes remain compatible; the changes make existing Hypervel unions and defaults truthful.
- **Implementation:** Cookie facade request reads now normalize enum keys and apply mixed defaults at the facade boundary without passing them into Request's narrower API. CookieJar and QueueingFactory expose nested request arrays and array defaults truthfully, queued lookup exposes its existing mixed fallback, and queued-cookie element types remain precise across contract, concrete, and facade metadata. The contract duration text and public selective-encryption guidance now match the implemented 400-day and opt-in behavior. No package dependency or state architecture changed.
- **Regression tests:** A package-local facade test covers unit, string-backed, integer-backed, and zero-backed names; missing-key mixed fallback; present-value precedence over a supplied object default; and the unchanged all-cookie read. Jar coverage proves nested arrays, array defaults, and non-Cookie queued sentinels. Existing middleware regressions continue to pin selective-encryption precedence.
- **Validation and review:** Changed tests, the complete Cookie suite, focused Cookie/contracts/facade PHPStan, PHP CS Fixer, both full PHPStan configurations, the complete parallel suite, both Testbench suites, `git diff --check`, stale-signature scans, and a fresh caller/callee, fallback, enum-zero, state-lifetime, performance, and overengineering review are green. Independent code review added the present-value-with-default assertion and signed off with no remaining finding.
- **Laravel-facing result:** Public Laravel call shapes and configuration remain compatible. Existing Hypervel enum and mixed-default surfaces now behave as declared, nested cookie arrays no longer fail at an over-narrow return type, and the existing Hypervel selective-encryption option is documented rather than redesigned.
- **Assessment:** The accepted design fixes six demonstrated type/documentation defects at their existing boundaries and adds no concurrency machinery or speculative robustness. Worker-shared defaults and coroutine-local queue ownership remain unchanged.
- **Owner pre-commit review:** The owner reviewed the completed work-unit summary and approved committing the signed-off implementation.

### Correct Engine stream, WebSocket, and Swoole ownership

- **Architecture and inspected risk surfaces:** Engine is Hypervel's low-level Swoole transport package, ported from Hyperf Engine but used only with Swoole. The audit covered every Engine source and test file; contracts, package dependencies, provider bindings, HTTP/2 and WebSocket clients, native Swoole return/error behavior, Stream's PSR contract, websocket-server consumers, current local Hyperf Engine and multi-engine sources, and all repository callers of the copied HTTP portability layer.

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `engine-02` | Defect | Minor | High | Stream accepts negative reads, remains operable after detachment, returns a false size, and throws for PSR metadata queries | Model detachment explicitly, reject invalid reads, enforce detached-operation failures, and return the truthful PSR size and metadata results without introducing cursor/seek semantics |
| `engine-03` | Defect | Major | High | HTTP/2 construction ignores a failed native connection and exposes an unusable client | Check the native connect result and throw the existing `HttpClientException` with Swoole's error details |
| `engine-04` | Defect | Major | High | WebSocket response push always reports success even when either native Swoole push returns false | Return the native boolean from both supported response/server branches |
| `engine-05` | Defect | Major | High | WebSocket construction ignores failed upgrade and callback failures skip connection-local cleanup | Reject failed upgrades and place the existing connection/event cleanup in a single per-connection `finally` boundary |
| `engine-06` | Defect | Minor | High | Frame exposes dead payload-length state, an ignored serialization argument, and a masking-key API that Swoole cannot honor | Remove the dead/misleading surface and model only the native boolean mask flag while retaining computed payload length |
| `engine-07` | Improvement | Improvement | High | A copied Hyperf HTTP server/factory/emitter/event-stream layer has no consumers because Hypervel permanently supports Swoole only and its live servers use the canonical HTTP bridges | Delete the accidental multi-engine portability carryover, its bindings/dependencies/tests, and stale plan references; document Engine's Swoole-only architecture |

- **Important rejected concerns:** `Coroutine::pid(0)` is correct: Swoole treats coroutine ID zero as the current coroutine, and direct probes proved the proposed change would be a no-op inside a coroutine and less accurate outside one. Do not add generalized channel state, retry, timeout, lifecycle, acknowledgement, transport, or pooling machinery; destructors for native handles; deep Frame stream cloning; a custom WebSocket packer; shared-client synchronization; or a cursor/seek Stream redesign. Internal PONG failure remains observable through the next receive and does not gain a second policy path.
- **Cross-package implications:** Contracts owns the truthful Frame surface; websocket-server consumes Engine's native push boolean and is revalidated. The deleted HTTP portability layer has no source consumer: Hypervel's server, websocket-server, and Reverb stacks already use the Swoole-specific `RequestBridge` and `ResponseBridge` from http-server. No completed package assumption changes.
- **Approved API and architecture decision:** The owner confirmed that Hypervel is permanently Swoole-only and that Swow or interchangeable server engines will never be supported. Removing the unused Hyperf portability layer is therefore an approved greenfield simplification, not a Laravel-facing divergence. Frame's public Hyperf-specific surface is reduced only where the current methods are dead or misleading; no Laravel public API or configuration changes.
- **Regression strategy:** Pin detached Stream behavior, negative/zero reads, size, metadata, HTTP/2 connection failure, both native WebSocket push branches, failed upgrade, callback-failure cleanup, and Frame payload/mask/serialization behavior. Run Engine plus affected websocket-server and API-client tests, relevant static analysis, then the complete gate and a fresh caller/callee and stale-reference review.
- **Performance and complexity:** Stream adds only necessary detached/length guards. HTTP/2 and WebSocket upgrade checks occur once at connection creation. WebSocket cleanup uses one `try/finally` per connection, not per frame. Native push returns its existing boolean directly, Frame becomes smaller, and deleting the portability layer removes code and dependencies. No request-wide lock, registry, state machine, cache, retry, allocation class, service resolution, context slot, yield, or polling loop is added.
- **Implementation:** Stream now enforces its detached and length contracts; HTTP/2 construction rejects failed native connections; WebSocket push, upgrade, and connection-local cleanup preserve native results and ownership; Frame models only Swoole's real payload and mask capabilities. The unused HTTP server, factory, response emitter, event stream, their contracts and binding, obsolete package dependencies, and their dead regression test were removed together. Engine's README now records the permanent Swoole-only architecture, and the superseded HTTP-overload claims were removed from the earlier lifecycle plan.
- **Validation and review:** Focused Engine, websocket-server, and API-client tests passed; Engine package metadata validated; two complete `composer fix` runs passed; package/checklist comparison, stale-symbol scans, caller/callee tracing, and `git diff --check` were clean. Independent post-implementation review re-ran focused tests and static analysis, verified every changed source/test file and deletion, traced the live server architecture, and signed off with no findings.
- **Laravel-facing result:** Public Laravel APIs and configuration are unchanged. The removed contracts and Frame methods were Hyperf-derived Engine surfaces with no active repository consumer; their removal is the owner-approved Swoole-only simplification recorded above.
- **Final assessment:** The work fixes verified native-boundary and lifecycle defects while deleting more code than it adds. It introduces no request-wide coordination or per-frame machinery, no meaningful hot-path regression, and no speculative abstraction or workaround.
- **Owner pre-commit review:** The owner reviewed the completed work-unit summary and approved committing the signed-off implementation.

### Make coroutine creation and copied context failure-safe

- **Architecture and inspected risk surfaces:** Coroutine is Hyperf-derived Swoole infrastructure whose high-level creation boundary catches fire-and-forget child failures, while Context supplies optional parent-state replication and the concurrency helpers reserve capacity or wait counts before spawning. The audit covered every Coroutine source and test file; current local Hyperf Coroutine source; Context copy semantics and every `ReplicableContext` implementation; Concurrency, Foundation testing, Filesystem, Scout, Schedule, Sentry, Telescope, WebSocket, and public-documentation consumers; native hook, channel, cancellation, and coroutine-context behavior; and the completed Context audit decision that replication failure had previously been treated as non-transactional.

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `coroutine-01` | Defect | Minor | High | `run()` splats valid callable arrays into separate arguments, leaks requested Swoole hook flags on failure, and replaces rather than restores prior flags on success | Distinguish callable arrays from callback/argument tuples, restore the exact prior hook flags in `finally`, and keep the correction at the cold non-coroutine root boundary |
| `coroutine-02` | Defect | Major | High | A container or exception-handler failure during child reporting escapes the high-level coroutine root and can terminate the worker; Concurrent duplicates the same throwable reporting path | Make Coroutine reporting no-throw with a best-effort original-error fallback, and delete both duplicate Concurrent catches plus their private reporting path |
| `coroutine-03` | Defect | Minor | High | `Locker::unlock()` and `Mutex::clear()` retain every released dynamic key as a null worker-static map entry | Remove released keys before closing their captured channels and narrow the maps to their truthful channel-only type |
| `coroutine-04` | Defect | Minor | High | `Channel\Caller::initInstance()` destroys a healthy published instance before its replacement factory succeeds | Build the replacement instance and channel locally, publish only after success, then close the prior channel |
| `coroutine-05` | Defect | Major | High | Filesystem and LockableFile unconditionally unlock in `finally`, so canceling a waiter that never acquired ownership releases another coroutine's gate | Record acquisition in one local boolean at each owning callback boundary and unlock only the exact acquired gate; retain Locker's distinct single-flight semantics and add no callback abstraction |
| `coroutine-06` | Defect | Major | High | `Coroutine::fork()` replicates parent context inside the child before caller cleanup begins, so a throwing user `replicate()` is reported as child failure while Concurrent capacity and Parallel/Concurrency wait counts remain stranded | Capture and replicate the source map transactionally in the parent before spawning, merge it in the child, and preserve one source plus one target selection without a handshake, registry, lock, retry, or per-consumer copy implementation |
| `coroutine-07` | Defect | Major | High | `WaitConcurrent` inherits `fork()` without adding the forked child to its wait group, so `wait()` can report completion while work is still running | Override `fork()` with the same add, child-finally, and synchronous-failure rollback contract as `create()` |
| `foundation-02` | Defect | Minor | High | Foundation's testing Waiter performs replicated context copy before registering its result defer, so replication failure becomes a misleading timeout | Capture parent context synchronously and install it in the child before registering result delivery |
| `websocket-server-01` | Defect | Minor | High | WebSocket context copy reads an absent source file descriptor through an unchecked raw array offset | Treat a missing source descriptor as a no-op, matching CoroutineContext copy behavior |

- **Important rejected concerns:** Do not add a `Locker::block()` callback API: Locker intentionally supports both single-flight gate use, where waiters skip work, and the two Filesystem serialization loops, so centralizing the latter would blur semantics merely to deduplicate two small sites. Do not add context handshakes, snapshot objects, registries, locks, retries, generalized cancellation, automatic Waiter child cancellation, reusable-executor synchronization, Mutex misuse recovery, channel health checks, or deadlines around internally owned joins. Keep non-replicable copied objects shared and retain every consumer-less public Hyperf primitive unless a concrete removal benefit is established.
- **Context revalidation:** `coroutine-06` supersedes only the completed Context audit's rejected claim that user `replicate()` failure could remain non-transactional. `CoroutineContext::captureFrom(array $keys = [], ?int $fromCoroutineId = null)` becomes the one source-selection and replication owner, defaults to the current coroutine, and returns an empty array outside a coroutine without reading worker-global fallback storage. `copyFrom()` keeps its existing public signature, delegates through `captureFrom()`, and targets the explicit current coroutine so it cannot write into fallback storage. `setMany()` preserves empty-input no-op, integer-key normalization, explicit dead-target failure, and non-coroutine fallback while selecting the destination once. Replication now occurs in the forking coroutine at fork invocation, which is the natural snapshot boundary and matches every current synchronous, side-effect-free `ReplicableContext` implementation.
- **Cross-package implications:** Context owns transactional capture. Coroutine owns spawn timing, reporting, capacity, wait tracking, hook state, and the Locker/Mutex maps. Filesystem owns its two acquisition booleans. Foundation owns its testing Waiter. WebSocket Server owns absent-descriptor handling. Concurrency consumes the corrected synchronous fork-failure contract; later full audits of Concurrency, Foundation, Filesystem, and WebSocket Server must retain and revalidate these boundaries.
- **Regression strategy:** Cover callable-array and callback-tuple execution plus exact hook restoration; process survival and original diagnostic when the exception handler throws; released Locker and Mutex key removal; failed Caller replacement preserving the prior instance; waiter cancellation never unlocking a live Filesystem owner in both caller shapes; synchronous replication failure and balanced Concurrent, Parallel, Concurrency-driver, and WaitConcurrent bookkeeping; WaitConcurrent waiting for ordinary forked work; Foundation Waiter surfacing replication failure; and missing WebSocket source copy preserving destination state. Keep one regression per distinct ownership invariant rather than duplicating leaf permutations.
- **Documentation:** Add Coroutine's Hyperf upstream reference and the standard boot-only warning to `afterCreated()`. Document the new public `captureFrom()` transfer snapshot, key filtering, explicit source targeting, replication timing, and non-serialization boundary in the existing coroutine-context guide; internal bug fixes do not receive user-facing prose.
- **Performance and compatibility:** Public Laravel APIs and configuration are unchanged. `captureFrom()` is a Hypervel/Hyperf infrastructure addition with demonstrated cross-package consumers. Successful fork retains the existing source-map allocation and one source plus one destination lookup, while current-source defaulting removes the redundant coroutine-ID round trip; replication merely moves immediately before spawn. Other changes are cold, failure-only, retained-memory reductions, or the bookkeeping already promised by `WaitConcurrent`. No meaningful hot-path regression or speculative abstraction is accepted.
- **Implementation:** Context now owns transactional parent-side capture and one-pass destination installation. Coroutine performs capture before spawn, contains reporter failures, restores exact native hook state, and removes duplicate Concurrent reporting. WaitConcurrent tracks forked work; Locker and Mutex remove released keys; Caller publishes replacements only after successful construction; both Filesystem atomic boundaries release only gates they acquired; Foundation's testing Waiter captures before allocating its result channel; and WebSocket copying ignores absent sources. Superseded comments and direct-child-copy wording were removed.
- **Regression tests:** Deterministic regressions cover callable arrays, callback tuples, hook restoration, reporter failure, transactional replication, current/all and filtered context capture, named and positional explicit sources, dead sources, exclusion of non-coroutine fallback storage, balanced capacity and wait counts across every consumer, ordinary WaitConcurrent fork completion, canceled Filesystem waiters, failed Caller replacement, released-key removal, Foundation Waiter failure propagation, and missing WebSocket sources. Failure-path tests use bounded observation and explicit cleanup so a regression fails instead of hanging the suite.
- **Validation and review:** Every changed test file and the affected Context, Coroutine, Concurrency, Filesystem, Foundation testing, Log, and WebSocket groups are green. PHP CS Fixer, both PHPStan configurations, the complete parallel suite, both Testbench suites, `git diff --check`, package-checklist parity, stale-reference scans, a fresh caller/callee and lifecycle review, and independent code review are complete.
- **Laravel-facing result:** Public Laravel APIs, configuration, documented behavior, and conventional extension patterns are unchanged. The only new public method is the Hypervel/Hyperf infrastructure primitive `CoroutineContext::captureFrom()`, which has concrete framework consumers and centralizes an existing operation rather than introducing a new user-facing model.
- **Assessment:** The final design fixes demonstrated creation, reporting, ownership, wait-tracking, retained-state, and context-copy defects at their lowest boundaries. It adds no lock, registry, retry, handshake, timeout policy, cancellation framework, or compatibility layer; removes duplicate reporting and stale map entries; and adds no meaningful hot-path cost.
- **Later Filesystem revalidation:** The full Filesystem audit removed its worker-local `Locker` layer and the two acquisition booleans introduced for `coroutine-05`, replacing them with checked native file-lock ownership. Cancellation can no longer release another coroutine's worker-local gate because no such gate remains. Coroutine's own Locker semantics and regressions are unchanged.
- **Later WebSocket Server revalidation:** The full WebSocket Server audit retained the `websocket-server-01` missing-source no-op and extended the same context boundary with dotted deletion, key-existence semantics, and null-preserving filtered copying. Focused context coverage and the complete gate remain green.

### Make process concurrency transport lossless and reconstruct failures safely

- **Architecture and inspected risk surfaces:** Concurrency is a Laravel-derived facade and driver package with a Hypervel coroutine driver as its default. The audit covered every Concurrency source and test file; its contract, facade, configuration, README, and public guide; the Foundation hidden serialized-closure command; both response decoders in Concurrency and Testbench; Process pool/start/wait behavior; every repository caller; current local Laravel source, tests, documentation, and originating timeout/gzip fixes; and the completed `coroutine-06` and `support-02` boundaries.

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `concurrency-01` | Defect | Major | High | The hidden command embeds binary PHP serialization directly in JSON, so valid non-UTF-8 results cannot cross the process boundary; its failure reporter and failure envelope can also replace the original task failure | Base64 only the serialized success result; strictly decode it at both consumers; contain best-effort reporting; encode the normal failure envelope with recursive invalid-UTF-8 substitution; and retain one fixed reconstructible ASCII `RuntimeException` fallback whose diagnostic class name is byte-escaped safely |
| `concurrency-02` | Defect | Major | High | Appended gzip output bytes corrupt both JSON decoders | Port Laravel's trim-at-first-gzip-magic boundary to both decoders and use throwing JSON decode so malformed protocol output fails directly |
| `concurrency-03` | Defect | Minor | High | Truthiness filtering drops false, zero, empty-string, and null constructor values; null placeholders override inaccessible optional defaults; empty-parameter message fallback cannot reconstruct classes with inaccessible non-string constructor state | Transport initialized public constructor-backed values by name; emit declared defaults for inaccessible optional parameters, omit variadics without defaults, and advertise `RuntimeException` when required state is inaccessible; reconstruct structurally with named arguments in both decoders |
| `concurrency-04` | Improvement | Improvement | High | Hypervel lacks current Laravel's optional task timeout API | Owner approved current-Laravel parity: add `CarbonInterval\|int\|null $timeout = null` to the contract, facade, and all three drivers; apply it only to Process pending tasks and deliberately leave coroutine/sync execution unchanged |
| `concurrency-05` | Documentation defect | Minor | High | The public guide overstates copied-context isolation by implying mutation of an ordinary shared object reference cannot be observed | State the exact contract: the context map is copied, ordinary object references remain shared, and `ReplicableContext` values are copied during capture |
| `concurrency-06` | Documentation | Minor | High | The package README has neither Laravel provenance nor its material architecture differences | Add the Laravel Concurrency source reference and only the coroutine-default/no-fork public differences |
| `concurrency-07` | Test isolation defect | Minor | High | The hidden-command environment regression restores its server variable only after assertions | Preserve prior presence and value and restore them exactly in `finally` |

- **Exception reconstruction boundary:** Missing constructors and the unchanged native `Exception`/`Error` constructor transport the original message as a named argument. Direct and inherited constructors declared anywhere else use the same extraction rules: every variadic is omitted before property inspection; an initialized public property matching a non-variadic constructor parameter transports its actual value; an inaccessible optional parameter with a declared default transports that default by name; and an inaccessible required parameter makes the original class unreconstructible. Before advertising the original class, encode and decode the parameter map with the exact final JSON flags and require strict identity, so objects, enums, nested object state, binary strings, and other type-changing values degrade to `RuntimeException` plus the original message instead of producing a parent-side `TypeError` or silent state mutation. Invalid UTF-8 class names degrade through the same normal envelope. Truly unencodable state uses the fixed ASCII fallback. Both decoders always construct structurally from the parameter map, so a genuine empty array means zero arguments, and contain remaining parent-only construction failures as `RuntimeException` with the construction error as its previous exception. Reconstructible types are favored over exact message fidelity: inaccessible optional and variadic state is replaced with defaults or omitted, while the subprocess sends the original exception through best-effort reporting before transport. Do not read private/protected state, infer message parameters, mutate exception internals, maintain an exception-class registry, or add a remote-exception hierarchy.
- **Important rejected concerns:** Do not add automatic child cancellation, coroutine timeout/cancellation policy, process-pool rollback machinery, protocol DTOs or versioning, a shared protocol service, recursive parameter encoders, task registries, retries, deep context copying, arbitrary wait deadlines, reporter abstractions, or manager/cache redesign. The repository has no production cancellation path for a synchronous Concurrency caller, Symfony Process teardown owns local partial-start unwind, and the three small transport boundaries do not justify a cross-package abstraction.
- **Owner approvals:** The owner approved always-base64 transport for the serialized subprocess result after reviewing its one encode/decode and approximately one-third temporary result-field expansion. This cost applies only when a process task returns, not to normal requests, jobs, or coroutine concurrency. The owner also approved the current-Laravel timeout API despite its public Driver-contract expansion; Process alone enforces it, matching Laravel's process-only behavior.
- **Performance and compatibility:** No normal request, job, or coroutine path changes. Process results gain one base64 encode/decode and temporary field expansion; both decoders gain one linear gzip-marker scan beside their existing linear JSON decode. Failure transport performs one extra JSON round trip before advertising a custom exception class, and reconstruction/fallback work remains failure-only. Timeout configuration occurs only while building explicit process tasks. No lock, context slot, worker cache, retained state, service resolution, registry, retry, yield, or polling loop is added. Laravel-facing timeout parity is restored; the binary-result and falsey-constructor defects are shared by current Laravel and are fixed without changing ordinary call shapes.
- **Regression strategy:** Cover exact invalid-UTF-8 success round trips at the command and both decoders; appended gzip bytes and malformed JSON at both decoders; contained reporter failure; invalid-UTF-8 and non-JSON-native failure state; guaranteed ASCII fallback class diagnostics; public false/zero/empty/null/float parameters; inaccessible optional defaults; inaccessible required values; zero-argument and stored/un-stored variadics reconstructed with zero variadic elements; object, enum, nested-object, and binary-string parameter degradation; the deliberate class-versus-message fidelity boundary; parent-only missing classes, constructor failures, and non-Throwable advertised classes; timeout propagation only to Process; exact environment restoration after success/failure; and the retained enum/context-copy regressions. Run changed files immediately, focused Concurrency/Foundation/Testbench/Coroutine tests, `composer fix`, then a fresh caller/callee, protocol, public API, performance, stale-code, and overengineering review.
- **Implementation:** The hidden command now base64-wraps successful serialized values, contains reporter failure, and emits either a structurally verified exception envelope or one fixed ASCII fallback. ProcessDriver and Testbench's ProcessResult trim appended gzip output, decode JSON and base64 strictly, reconstruct named constructor state, and contain missing, invalid, non-Throwable, or failing parent-side classes as `RuntimeException`. The Driver contract, facade, and all drivers accept Laravel's optional timeout, with Process alone applying it. The environment regression restores exact prior state, the public guide states the real copied-context semantics and timeout behavior, the package README records provenance and material runtime differences, and the intentional ForkDriver omission is marked at its upstream source slot.
- **Regression tests:** The command and both decoders cover binary success values, gzip suffixes, malformed protocol data, reporter failure, UTF-8 and JSON edge cases, ASCII fallback, falsey and floating constructor values, defaulted and required hidden state, zero-argument and variadic constructors, inherited constructors, type-changing object/enum/binary state, missing and failing parent constructors, and non-Throwable advertised classes. Additional coverage verifies Process-only timeout application, current coroutine and enum behavior, exact environment restoration, and the real Testbench subprocess round trip.
- **Validation and review:** Every changed test file and the affected Concurrency, Foundation command, Coroutine, and Testbench process groups are green. PHP CS Fixer, both PHPStan configurations, the complete parallel suite, both Testbench suites, `git diff --check`, a fresh caller/callee, protocol, API, performance, stale-code, and overengineering review, and independent code review are complete. The final review independently reran the three central test files and signed off after verifying the untrusted non-Throwable guard and cross-package revalidation records.
- **Process revalidation:** `ProcessDriver` remains correct with `process-02` pool startup rollback, exhaustive failure cleanup, empty-pool support, and keyed result preservation; all 47 focused Concurrency tests pass against the corrected Process lifecycle.
- **Laravel-facing result:** Current Laravel's optional process-task timeout API is restored without changing existing calls; Coroutine and Sync retain their established behavior. Public configuration, documented call shapes, and conventional extension patterns are otherwise unchanged. The binary transport and exception-reconstruction corrections harden shared upstream behavior without removing or renaming any public API.
- **Assessment:** The final design fixes demonstrated transport, failure-reporting, reconstruction, timeout-parity, documentation, and test-isolation defects at the three existing protocol boundaries. It adds no DTO, registry, shared decoder service, retry, lock, context state, cancellation framework, or normal request/job/coroutine cost. The accepted process-only encoding and scan work is bounded to explicit process results and is the smallest complete correction.

### Release cleared coordinator timers deterministically

- **Architecture and inspected risk surfaces:** Coordinator is a Hyperf-derived worker-lifecycle package. `Coordinator` is a one-shot channel-backed latch, `CoordinatorManager` owns worker-lifetime named latches, and `Timer` runs one coroutine per callback against a captured coordinator. The audit covered every Coordinator source and test file; Engine channel close, timeout, and non-exception cancellation behavior; Coroutine creation hooks; every timer and coordinator consumer in Pool, Object Pool, Database, Redis, Queue, Reverb, HTTP, WebSocket, Core, and test cleanup; Pool-owned frequency lifecycle and teardown; and current Hyperf source and tests.

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `coordinator-01` | Defect | Major | High | `Timer::clear()` and `clearAll()` remove callback flags but leave timer coroutines blocked until their timeout or worker exit, retaining each coroutine and captured object graph | Retain each timer's coroutine ID and whether it is currently blocked in Coordinator; clearing removes registration and non-exception-cancels only a waiting timer, never a yielding user callback |
| `coordinator-02` | Defect | Minor | High | Coordinator directly uses `Hypervel\Coroutine\go()` and publicly types `Psr\Log\LoggerInterface` without declaring either package dependency | Add direct `hypervel/coroutine` and `psr/log` requirements to the Coordinator package metadata |
| `coordinator-03` | Test liveness defect | Minor | High | Coordinator tests assert inside child coroutines before decrementing their wait groups, so a regression can strand the parent indefinitely after the high-level coroutine boundary reports the assertion | Capture child results, guarantee `done()` in `finally`, and assert after the parent joins |
| `coordinator-04` | Documentation defect | Minor | High | The package lacks its Hyperf provenance, and the required timer-clear behavior intentionally differs from Hyperf's unset-only implementation | Add the upstream source reference and a concise source marker at the cancellation boundary explaining the retained-coroutine invariant |
| `pool-01` | Defect | Major | High | `ConstantFrequency`'s timer callback retains the frequency and its pool, so its destructor cannot clear the timer; closing a supported custom pool leaves the timer, callback, and pool graph alive until worker exit | Add a separate clearable-frequency capability, clear it deterministically from `Pool::close()` before draining connections, and remove the ineffective destructor without changing `LowFrequencyInterface` |
| `pool-02` | Maintainability | Minor | High | `KeepaliveConnection`'s timer callback retains the connection, so its destructor cannot run while cleanup is useful and only repeats the supported `close()` path after the timer is gone | Remove the dead destructor; retain `close()` as the explicit connection and heartbeat teardown boundary |
| `database-01` | Maintainability | Minor | High | `DbPool`'s heartbeat callback retains the pool, making its destructor unreachable while the timer is live and redundant after `close()` or callback self-termination | Remove the dead destructor; retain `close()` and `clearHeartbeat()` as the deterministic owner path, and revalidate during the later Database audit |
| `redis-01` | Maintainability | Minor | High | `RedisPool` has the same unreachable heartbeat destructor and already clears or self-stops the timer through explicit lifecycle paths | Remove the dead destructor; retain `close()` and `clearHeartbeat()` as the deterministic owner path; revalidated by the full Redis audit |

- **Timer ownership boundary:** A timer entry starts with a zero coroutine-ID sentinel before spawn, publishes the real child ID after successful creation, and removes itself in the child `finally`. A separate waiting marker is set only around an actual `Coordinator::yield()` call. This lets another coroutine wake a timer parked in framework-owned waiting while ensuring `clear()` never injects cancellation into a user callback that yields. The child rechecks registration before waiting or invoking the callback, covering re-entrant creation hooks without adding a handshake or publication protocol. `clearAll()` snapshots registered IDs and delegates to the same exact-owner cleanup.
- **Pool ownership boundary:** `ConstantFrequency`'s timer child retains its callback, which retains the frequency and pool, so the destructor cannot become reachable while live cleanup is still possible. `Pool::close()` is the supported terminal owner boundary: it establishes the closed state, clears only strategies implementing the additive `ClearableFrequencyInterface`, then closes the channel and drains idle connections. A custom strategy's cleanup failure is reported through the existing contained reporter and cannot abort the independent channel and connection teardown; the idempotency guard would otherwise make that partial close unrecoverable. Resource-free and custom `LowFrequencyInterface` implementations remain unchanged. Abandoning a pool without calling `close()` remains outside the supported lifecycle; breaking that reference cycle would require a disproportionate redesign and neither the rejected callback self-poll nor the old destructor would solve it.
- **Dead destructor boundary:** Keepalive, database-pool, and Redis-pool timer callbacks also retain their owners, so their destructors cannot run while the timer needs cleanup. Each already has an explicit `close()` path that clears its timer, and the database/Redis callbacks also self-stop during worker closure. Delete those destructors rather than preserving misleading no-op safety nets; do not add a replacement garbage-collection mechanism.
- **Important rejected concerns:** Do not make manager `clear()` or `initialize()` resume live coordinators, remove worker-start coordination, redesign diagnostic statistics, contain persistently throwing loggers with another reporter layer, validate speculative NaN/INF timeout inputs, add per-timer channels, replace Coordinator timers with native Swoole timers, or create a generic cancellation registry. Do not add an already-closed fast path to `Coordinator::yield()` for the measured approximately 76 ns saving; it is benchmark noise relative to request execution and does not justify divergence from Hyperf. Do not add Timer destructor guards, concrete `ConstantFrequency` coupling, reflective lifecycle checks, `clear()` to every low-frequency strategy, or delayed callback polling; the optional Pool-owned capability is the complete supported teardown.
- **Deferred Redis improvement:** Redis `CommandInvoker` can later replace its per-message watchdog timer with the channel's native push timeout, removing one timer coroutine per message. This is a meaningful Redis-owned simplification but has no remaining correctness urgency after `coordinator-01`; retain it for the Redis package audit and verify worker-exit interruption there.
- **Performance and compatibility:** Existing public methods, signatures, timeout behavior, and configuration remain unchanged. Normal timer registration gains two small integer/boolean map entries; clearing a still-waiting timer performs one cancellation that releases retained state immediately. Pool closure gains one cold-path capability check and only resource-owning strategies perform cleanup. User callbacks, normal requests without timers, connection borrow/release paths, and unrelated coroutine operations gain no work. The additive Hypervel-specific interface reuses `ConstantFrequency`'s existing public `clear()` method and does not change the Hyperf-shaped low-frequency contract.
- **Regression strategy:** Prove that clearing one or all long-lived timers releases their coroutines and returns statistics to zero after an explicit scheduler handoff; clearing during a yielding callback does not interrupt it; creation-hook re-entry remains leak-free; self-clear still exits without another interval; and spawn failure still rolls back registration. Prove that closing a real `ConstantFrequency`-backed pool releases its timer coroutine and prevents later periodic work. Prove that a throwing custom frequency cleanup is reported while channel closure and idle connection/bookkeeping teardown still complete. Move wait-group assertions to the parent and guarantee child completion signaling in `finally`. Run every changed file immediately, all Coordinator and affected Pool tests, `composer fix`, then a fresh caller/callee, lifetime, API, performance, stale-code, and overengineering review.
- **Implementation:** Timer now retains the exact coroutine ID for each registration and a separate marker only while that coroutine is blocked in Coordinator, so clear operations release framework-owned waits without canceling yielding callbacks. Coordinator declares its direct dependencies and records its upstream provenance. Pool has an additive clearable-frequency capability; `Pool::close()` contains and reports strategy cleanup failures before closing the channel and draining idle connections, and `ConstantFrequency` participates through its existing `clear()` method. The ineffective destructors on `ConstantFrequency`, `KeepaliveConnection`, `DbPool`, and `RedisPool` are removed in favor of their existing deterministic close paths. Coordinator test children always signal completion before parent-side assertions.
- **Regression tests:** Coordinator coverage proves prompt release for one and all cleared timers, creation-hook re-entry, callback-yield safety, self-clear behavior, and spawn rollback. Pool coverage proves deterministic `ConstantFrequency` release and continued channel/connection teardown after a custom cleanup failure. Existing Pool, Database, Redis, and coroutine-creation regressions were updated to inspect the new timer registration state and continue to verify explicit heartbeat teardown.
- **Validation and review:** Every changed test file and the affected Coordinator, Pool, Coroutine, Database heartbeat, and Redis heartbeat groups are green. PHP CS Fixer, both PHPStan configurations, the complete parallel suite, both Testbench suites, `git diff --check`, a broad timer-holder/destructor sweep, a fresh caller/callee, lifecycle, API, performance, stale-code, and overengineering review, and independent code review are complete. The final review independently reran the central Coordinator, Pool, and heartbeat tests and signed off after verifying all four dead destructors were removed without artifacts or missed production subclasses.
- **Laravel-facing result:** Coordinator and Pool frequency strategies are Hypervel/Hyperf infrastructure rather than Laravel public APIs. Existing public calls and behavior remain intact; the only additions are the optional clearable-frequency capability and prompt release of explicitly cleared or closed timer owners.
- **Assessment:** The final design gives each timer registration exact ownership and makes explicit closure the sole resource boundary. It adds only two small timer maps and one cold Pool-close capability check, while deleting four misleading destructors. No generic cancellation registry, polling loop, per-timer channel, reflection, callback self-check, low-frequency contract expansion, or normal request/connection-borrow overhead was introduced; the accepted design is the smallest complete fix for the demonstrated retention and teardown failures.

### Release signal watchers deterministically at process exit

- **Architecture and inspected risk surfaces:** Signal is a Hyperf-derived worker/process-lifecycle package. Its auto-singletoned manager resolves configured handler objects once per process incarnation and spawns one coroutine per process-type/signal pair during lifecycle start. Before this correction, those watchers relied on timeout polling to observe lifecycle exit. The audit covered every Signal source, test, fixture, configuration, and metadata file; the Signal contract and sole production handler; Engine signal wait and coroutine cancellation; worker and custom-process start/exit dispatch; provider ordering and configuration reload; every repository caller; current and prior Hyperf/Hypervel sources; and focused Swoole 6.2.2 scheduling, cancellation, and callback-context probes.

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `signal-01` | Defect | Major | High | A configured watcher can remain blocked for the default five-second signal timeout after worker/process exit begins, exceeding the default three-second Swoole shutdown allowance and causing forced termination | Replace reversible polling state with terminal manager-owned cleanup: wait indefinitely, track only watchers parked in the native wait, and exception-cancel those exact waiters at exit without interrupting active handlers |
| `signal-02` | Userland footgun | Minor | High | The package has no provenance or lifecycle guidance, so applications can unknowingly replace Swoole's native `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT` shutdown behavior or share mutable handler state across concurrent signal coroutines | Document provenance, native shutdown ownership, coroutine-enabled lifecycle, worker-lifetime handler instances, and the requirement that handlers be coroutine-safe |
| `signal-03` | Improvement | Improvement | High | Signal tests omit known return types and retain a teardown that writes `null` into the non-coroutine fallback after the test coroutine and is then redundantly cleared by global cleanup | With owner approval, add the known test/fixture/callback return types and remove the misleading dead teardown |

- **Watcher ownership boundary:** `SignalManager::listen()` keeps created IDs local until each spawn succeeds so later creation failure can still cancel every partial watcher. Each child records its own ID only while blocked in `EngineSignal::wait()` and clears that marker in `finally` before invoking application handlers. Terminal `stop()` snapshots and exception-cancels only the currently parked IDs; active handlers finish normally and terminal state prevents another wait. A native `false` result is terminal because indefinite waits return it only for an error or non-exception cancellation, and retrying would busy-spin. Real Swoole probes confirmed that `WorkerExit` can cancel a parked waiter from non-coroutine context and coroutine-enabled custom-process teardown can do the same from its process coroutine.
- **Implementation:** Replaced reversible `setStopped(bool)`/`isStopped()` state with terminal `stop(): void`; retained one top-level stopped guard in `listen()`; replaced five-second polling with indefinite native waits; tracked only coroutine IDs currently parked in those waits; exception-canceled a snapshot of those exact IDs at process exit; treated a native `false` result as terminal to prevent an error busy-loop; and kept handler execution outside the cancellable waiter set so active handlers finish before the watcher exits. Partial-spawn rollback remains exception-safe. Initialization and listen validation now use strict exact process types, lifecycle listeners call `stop()`, provider callbacks and tests use known return types, the redundant wrong-context test teardown is gone, and lifecycle warnings explain boot-only initialization/listening and terminal stop. The obsolete `signal.timeout` configuration and all polling code were removed.
- **Important rejected concerns:** Do not add a general watcher registry, state machine, lock, channel, retry, configurable cancellation timeout, destructor, active-handler cancellation, combined process bitmask, idempotent repeated-listen support, signal-number validation layer, handler cloning/scoping, non-coroutine signal mechanism, handler-exception redesign, shared Coroutine redesign for a theoretical yielding creation hook, or a large Boost guide. A per-spawn stopped recheck is also unnecessary because the production creation path cannot interleave lifecycle teardown inside the parent loop.
- **Regression tests:** Focused tests prove prompt release of one real native waiter, snapshot release of multiple parked waiters, completion of an already-running handler before its watcher exits, no watcher creation after terminal stop, cancellation of an earlier watcher after partial creation failure, both deregistration event paths, priority ordering, and replacement rather than accumulation on reinitialization. Native/process-isolated behavior and existing seams cover the lifecycle without a production test hook. The final Signal suite passes with 13 tests and 30 assertions.
- **Performance and complexity:** No request path changes. Startup retains at most one integer marker per configured signal while waiting, signal delivery performs only marker removal/re-entry around existing handler work, and teardown snapshots the bounded parked set. Indefinite waits delete periodic coroutine wakeups and config reads, so steady-state watcher work decreases. Documentation and test cleanup have no runtime cost. The parked-only map is the minimum state needed to distinguish framework-owned waits from yielding application handlers.
- **Owner approval:** The owner approved `signal-03` after reviewing its test-only benefit, churn, alternatives, parity effect, and overengineering assessment. Verified defect and documentation corrections otherwise met the plan's direct-implementation criteria.
- **Laravel-facing result:** No Laravel public API, configuration, documented behavior, or extension pattern changes. Signal is Hypervel/Hyperf-derived infrastructure rather than a Laravel port; Hyperf API parity is not required. The removed `SignalManager::setStopped()`/`isStopped()` surface and published `signal.timeout` key were Hypervel-specific. Applications still defining `signal.timeout` retain only an unused setting, while supported lifecycle registration now uses terminal `stop()`.
- **Validation and review:** `composer fix` completed with zero formatter changes, both PHPStan configurations green, 23,186 component tests and 65,992 assertions passing with 1,600 expected skips, 346 Testbench contract tests and 1,029 assertions passing with 3 expected skips, and 4 dogfood tests and 7 assertions passing. `composer validate --strict src/signal/composer.json`, `git diff --check`, package-checklist parity, and repository-wide stale-reference scans were clean. A Swoole 6.2.2 process probe verified that a custom `SIGTERM` waiter consumes the signal and leaves the worker alive, grounding the README warning. Fresh self-review traced every watcher lifecycle, caller, failure path, public/config surface, retained state, and hot-path effect; independent post-implementation review signed off after the multiple-waiter regression and native-signal documentation evidence were added.
- **Assessment:** All three accepted findings are closed. The implementation removes polling and obsolete reversible state while adding only the bounded ownership map required for exact cancellation. It adds no request-hot-path work, no registry abstraction, lock, channel, retry, timeout, destructor, compatibility layer, or handler-interruption path, and leaves no known stale or workaround code.

### Bound pool resources and connection progress deterministically

- **Architecture and inspected risk surfaces:** Pool is Hyperf-derived connection infrastructure with a worker-lifetime abstract pool, explicit managed/borrowed ownership, one canonical idle queue, optional frequency maintenance, a heartbeat-capable connection extension surface, and a callback-driven SimplePool extension surface. Database and Redis are its two production consumers and own their native connection construction, heartbeat, idle, lifetime, and event-duration boundaries. The audit covered every Pool source and test file; Pool contracts and split-package metadata; Coordinator timer and Engine channel/spawn behavior; every Database and Redis pool, connection, connector, config, test, and documentation consumer; current Hyperf Pool and consumer patterns; prior Pool lifecycle hardening; and focused spawn, heartbeat, frequency, configuration, and clock probes.

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `pool-03` | Defect | Minor | High | Retained `KeepaliveConnection` extension subclasses publish an acquired resource and connected state before heartbeat timer creation commits, and heartbeat failure clears state without closing the queued resource | Reuse the existing close boundary on both failures, fall back to clear only when another call prevents close, and preserve the original creation or heartbeat failure |
| `pool-04` | Defect | Major | High | Documented `pool.connect_timeout` is never read by Database or Redis, so a dead network peer can hold a creating slot until the native or OS timeout despite the configured bound | Apply the validated option through native driver settings with explicit native configuration taking precedence: PDO MySQL/MariaDB timeout, PostgreSQL DSN timeout, and Redis top-level timeout; do not wrap factories in cancellation coroutines |
| `pool-05` | Defect | Minor | High | Pool, Database, and Redis idle and generation-lifetime comparisons use wall time, so clock steps can prematurely recycle or over-retain connections | Convert every read and write in each comparison group to direct monotonic float timestamps, including heartbeat sweeps and injected test timestamps, without a clock abstraction or additional reads |
| `database-02` | Defect | Minor | High | Database query duration and cumulative-threshold events use wall time and can report negative or incorrect elapsed values after a clock step | Convert the existing start/end reads to the same direct monotonic float primitive and revalidate query event and threshold behavior |
| `redis-02` | Defect | Minor | High | Redis success/failure command events measure elapsed time with wall time and can report negative or incorrect durations after a clock step | Convert the existing start/end reads to the same direct monotonic float primitive and revalidate both event paths |
| `pool-06` | Defect | Minor | High | `Frequency` retains offsets ten through zero for its configured ten-second window, averaging eleven buckets and shifting low-frequency decisions | Retain exactly `now - time + 1` through `now` and cover the expired eleventh-bucket result without adding a clock seam |
| `pool-07` | Defect | Minor | High | The retained Keepalive logger extension seam directly types `Psr\Log\LoggerInterface` while the split Pool package relies on a transitive dependency | Add a direct `psr/log` requirement; retain the broader protected logger seam |
| `pool-08` | Improvement | Improvement | High | An unused incomplete Context helper, an unused Frequency-to-Pool reference enforced by `LowFrequencyInterface`, and an empty Redis Frequency subclass retain dependencies, object cycles, and no-op differentiation without owning behavior | With owner approval, delete Context and its Context dependency, remove the false constructor requirement at `LowFrequencyInterface` and Frequency, and use Pool Frequency directly from Redis without an alias subclass |
| `pool-09` | Documentation defect | Minor | High | Pool lacks provenance and ownership guidance, while option and Boost documentation imply `min_connections` eagerly prewarms, replenishes, or guarantees idle capacity although it only bounds excess-idle trimming of the total managed count | Add concise provenance and lifecycle guidance and document the existing managed-count trim floor accurately; do not rename the key or add prewarming machinery |

- **Retained extension boundary:** Keep `KeepaliveConnection` and SimplePool because they are coherent, tested or evidenced public building blocks whose likely use and lifecycle are understood, not merely because Hyperf contains them. Hyperf NSQ demonstrates the keepalive subclass shape, Hyperf Guzzle and other current consumers demonstrate SimplePool, and this worktree's archived Guzzle handler also uses SimplePool. Add only proportionate SimplePool public-boundary coverage; do not add a registry, factory-wide close operation, destructor, or generic resource callback.
- **Minimum-connection boundary:** `min_connections` is a lower bound for excess-idle trimming of the total managed count, not an idle-count invariant or a guaranteed total minimum. Connections are created on demand, so the caller that first needs each new connection pays its establishment cost and the pool may have zero idle connections under load. Lifecycle-expired or unhealthy connections and explicit discards can reduce the managed count below the configured value; failed creation can leave it there. None is automatically replenished. This is inherited Hyperf behavior, so document it without a divergence note and do not add prewarming or replenishment machinery.
- **Connect-timeout boundary:** Pool owns the validated common option, while each consumer owns translation to the native connection primitive. MySQL and MariaDB inherit the same PDO options path and receive a rounded-up integer timeout only when `PDO::ATTR_TIMEOUT` is absent. PostgreSQL receives a rounded-up DSN `connect_timeout` only when its native key is absent. Redis receives the validated float as its top-level `timeout` only when caller configuration did not provide one; the existing connection config merge preserves that incoming value over the internal zero default. SQLite has no network connection establishment to bound. The existing default ten-second bound therefore becomes real for previously unbounded pooled network connects.
- **Monotonic boundary:** Use `hrtime(true) / 1e9` directly at every lifecycle and live event-duration read without introducing an injected clock or helper. Keep each comparison group in one domain, including creation timestamps and jittered lifetime deadlines. Do not convert Redis's cross-process wall-clock limiter, test-harness deadlines, Frequency's wall-clock buckets, or the Seeder's human-only duration display. Local repeated benchmarking measured monotonic reads marginally faster than `microtime(true)` with the same read count, so no hot-path regression is accepted.
- **Owner-approved improvements:** The owner approved deleting the unused Pool Context class and resulting `hypervel/context` dependency, deleting Frequency's unread Pool constructor argument/reference cycle, and deleting the empty Redis Frequency subclass. Implementation then exposed that the dead Pool argument is required by the public `LowFrequencyInterface` constructor contract. Focused second-opinion consensus and a separate owner addendum approved removing that false constructor requirement and the two empty test-stub constructors at the owning boundary; Frequency keeps only its timestamp initialization, while ConstantFrequency retains its real Pool constructor. The owner also approved retaining and hardening Keepalive and SimplePool under the complete-building-block principle above. No compatibility aliases or deprecated shims remain because these are Hypervel-specific, non-Laravel surfaces in the greenfield 0.4 line.
- **Important rejected concerns:** Do not alter the corrected Pool/Channel ownership model; null ConstantFrequency's Pool reference while a callback can be active; add destructors, weak references, GC hooks, timer or pool registries, locks, retries, state machines, per-pool channels, or generic spawn/cancel timeouts; add SimplePool factory teardown machinery; convert Frequency to monotonic time at borrow-path cost; change its bounded 60-versus-61-second low-frequency trigger; rename `min_connections`; or add eager prewarming. Do not move any frequency interface between Contracts and Pool: none has a consumer outside `hypervel/pool`, so namespace consolidation would add public churn without functional decoupling or another practical benefit. Hyperf parity is not a requirement, but verified complete extension surfaces are not deleted merely for having no current production subclass.
- **Performance and compatibility:** Keepalive cleanup is failure-only. Native timeout translation occurs at pool construction or native connection creation and adds no borrow/release work. Monotonic conversion keeps the existing number of clock reads and measured no regression. Frequency adds only the corrected integer window bound inside existing bucket maintenance. The approved deletions reduce dependency and retained-object surface. Removing the Hypervel-specific low-frequency constructor requirement broadens implementation freedom and adds no runtime work; no repository caller constructs strategies through that interface. No Laravel-facing API or configuration changes are involved; the existing Hypervel-specific timeout key and behavior are retained and corrected.
- **Regression strategy:** Prove Keepalive spawn and heartbeat failures close exactly once and leave disconnected state; prove native timeout propagation and explicit precedence for Database and Redis; prove SimplePool activity uses the monotonic domain; keep every injected lifecycle timestamp in that domain; revalidate Database query-duration thresholds and Redis success/failure event durations; prove the eleventh frequency bucket no longer affects the public result; run every changed test file immediately, all affected Pool/Database/Redis groups, `composer fix`, a fresh caller/callee and clock-domain review, and independent code review.
- **Implementation:** Keepalive creation and heartbeat failures now reuse the deterministic close boundary without replacing their primary failure. Database maps the validated connect timeout to native MySQL/MariaDB PDO options or the PostgreSQL DSN, Redis maps it to the native client timeout, and explicit native configuration wins. Pool, Database, and Redis lifecycle and live duration measurements now use one monotonic domain. Frequency retains exactly its configured ten buckets. Pool directly declares `psr/log`; the unused Context helper and Context dependency, Frequency-to-Pool constructor cycle, false low-frequency constructor contract, and empty Redis Frequency alias are removed. Pool provenance, ownership, timeout, and managed-count trim semantics are documented at the package, contract, option, and Database/Redis guide surfaces.
- **Cross-package revalidation:** The completed `pool-01` and `pool-02` lifecycle boundaries remain intact: Pool close still clears `ConstantFrequency` deterministically, and Keepalive teardown remains owned by explicit `close()` without a dead destructor.
- **Regression tests:** Focused coverage proves Keepalive creation and heartbeat failures close exactly once, every native timeout translation and precedence branch, monotonic SimplePool activity and callback-resource reuse, monotonic Database query duration, non-negative Redis success/failure event duration, lifecycle generation bounds, and exclusion of the expired eleventh frequency bucket. Every changed test file and the affected Pool, Database, and Redis groups are green; the final SimplePool review addition passes with one test and six assertions.
- **Validation and review:** Final `composer fix` completed with zero formatter changes, both PHPStan configurations green, 23,195 component tests and 66,046 assertions passing with 1,600 expected skips, 346 Testbench contract tests and 1,029 assertions passing with 3 expected skips, and 4 dogfood tests and 7 assertions passing. `composer validate --strict src/pool/composer.json`, `git diff --check`, broad deleted-symbol/implementer/clock-domain/documentation sweeps, and the package-checklist parity check are clean. Fresh self-review corrected the over-strong idle-floor documentation model and every same-family wording artifact. Independent post-implementation review traced the complete diff, requested the focused SimplePool reuse assertion, re-reviewed it after the final full gate, and signed off with no remaining concern.
- **Laravel-facing result:** No Laravel public API, configuration key or structure, documented behavior, or conventional extension pattern changes. Pool is Hyperf-derived infrastructure, and Hyperf API parity is not required. The existing Hypervel-specific `pool.connect_timeout` key now enforces its documented connection-establishment bound; the owner-approved low-frequency constructor removal broadens the Hypervel-specific extension contract without a compatibility shim.
- **Assessment:** All accepted Pool findings and the linked Database/Redis duration corrections are closed. The implementation adds work only during pool/native-client construction, existing clock reads, bucket maintenance, or failure cleanup; normal connection borrow/release gains no allocation, lookup, lock, yield, retry, logging, or extra clock read. The approved deletions reduce dependencies, retained object cycles, and empty API surface. No clock abstraction, cancellation wrapper, retry layer, prewarming/replenishment mechanism, registry, state machine, compatibility layer, or other speculative machinery remains.

### Isolate object-pool maintenance and remove false dependencies

- **Architecture and inspected risk surfaces:** Object Pool is Hypervel-specific worker-lifetime infrastructure with a singleton factory registry, lazily created bounded object pools, explicit lease release/discard ownership, periodic recycler maintenance, and contained cleanup reporting. The audit covered every source and test file; factory, pool, recycler, lease, proxy, Sentry transport, Filesystem, Queue, and Broadcasting consumers; split-package metadata; public Object Pool documentation; the completed Object Pool lifecycle design plan; current Hyperf references; and Engine channel, Coordinator timer, Coroutine creation, container, and test-cleanup boundaries.

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `object-pool-01` | Defect | Minor | High | One throwing public-contract pool aborts an entire recycler tick and indefinitely starves every later registered pool of expiry, trim, and idle eviction | Isolate each complete per-pool maintenance transaction, report the registry identity with the original failure chained, skip later methods only for that failed pool, and retain the outer catch for factory-snapshot failures |
| `object-pool-02` | Userland footgun | Minor | High | Factory flush and recycler configuration/lifecycle methods mutate singleton worker-wide state without consistently documenting their boot/test boundary at both contract and concrete surfaces | Add the standard warnings with concrete worker-wide consequences to `flush()`, `setInterval()`, `setTimer()`, `start()`, and `stop()`; leave runtime operations and pure readers unwarned |
| `object-pool-03` | Improvement | Improvement | High | Object Pool, SimpleObjectPool, PoolManager, and the Sentry pool retain and forward an unread application Container while the split package directly requires Carbon after its only user was deleted | With owner approval, remove the false constructor/property/import chain and every construction-site argument, drop only the stale direct Carbon requirement, and correct only the obsolete constructor snippets in the completed lifecycle plan |

- **Owner-approved improvement boundary:** The owner approved `object-pool-03` after reviewing its practical benefit, constructor churn, leave-as-is alternative, parity effect, hot-path effect, and overengineering assessment. Keep `hypervel/container` and `hypervel/contracts` because PoolErrorReporter, StartRecycler, and PoolManager's custom pool-factory resolution still legitimately use them. Remove no compatibility shim: these constructors are Hypervel-specific, Laravel parity is unaffected, and Hyperf API parity is not required.
- **Important rejected concerns:** Do not add manager locking, proxy pool caching or generation tracking, destructor/native-teardown changes, worker-exit flushing, protected-channel ownership assertions, generic discard-on-operation-exception behavior, discard-time activity stamping, speculative test barriers, concrete container aliases, per-method recovery within one failing pool, or a generic contextual reporting API. The current manager publishes only after synchronous construction; leases and proxies already own terminal cleanup; pool teardown is explicitly native-free where destructors can reach it; supported lifecycle closure is deterministic; and the recycler needs only one direct per-pool catch.
- **Cross-package implications:** Sentry construction sites and affected Object Pool consumers/tests must follow the constructor correction. No completed lower-level assumption changes and no later revalidation dependency is introduced.
- **Performance and compatibility:** Normal pool get, borrow, release, discard, proxy, request, job, and transport paths gain no work. Recycler maintenance gains one direct try/catch per registered pool at the existing ten-second interval; the identity wrapper allocates only after an actual failure. Constructor cleanup marginally reduces pool creation work and retained references. No Laravel-facing API, configuration, documented behavior, or conventional extension pattern changes.
- **Regression strategy:** Prove that a first pool failing in `sweepExpired()` is reported with its identity and exact original failure, does not continue to `trimIdle()`, and cannot prevent a second pool from completing all maintenance methods. Update constructor coverage and all affected callers, run each changed test file immediately, run focused Object Pool and affected consumer tests, validate the split manifest, run `composer fix`, then perform a fresh caller/callee, lifecycle, API, performance, stale-code, and overengineering review before independent code review.
- **Implementation:** PoolRecycler now isolates each complete pool maintenance transaction, reports an identity-named wrapper with the original failure chained, and documents why a throwing public-contract pool cannot be allowed to starve unrelated pools; the existing timer-level catch remains for factory-snapshot failure. Factory and Recycler contracts now carry the same worker-lifecycle warnings as their concretes. ObjectPool, SimpleObjectPool, PoolManager, and Sentry's transport Pool no longer retain or forward an application Container; every caller and test uses only the real constructor dependencies. The stale direct Carbon requirement is removed, while the still-used Container and Contracts dependencies remain. The completed Object Pool lifecycle plan describes the corrected constructors without altering its legitimate container behavior.
- **Regression tests:** The new recycler regression proves the old first-pool `sweepExpired()` failure is reported with its registry identity and exact original throwable, skips that pool's `trimIdle()`, and cannot prevent a later pool from completing `isIdle()`, `sweepExpired()`, and `trimIdle()`. Updated Object Pool, Filesystem, Queue, Broadcasting, and Sentry coverage exercises every corrected constructor and container-resolved PoolManager binding without weakening existing assertions.
- **Validation and review:** Every changed test file passed immediately. The Object Pool package passes with 168 tests and 411 assertions; Sentry with 241 tests and 673 assertions; focused Queue manager resolution with 10 tests and 86 assertions; and Broadcasting manager integration with 20 tests and 66 assertions. Final `composer fix` changed no formatted file, both PHPStan configurations are green, 23,196 component tests and 66,057 assertions pass with 1,600 expected skips, 346 Testbench contract tests and 1,029 assertions pass with 3 expected skips, and 4 dogfood tests and 7 assertions pass. The split manifest validates strictly, `git diff --check` and repository-wide stale-constructor/Carbon scans are clean, and fresh full-diff lifecycle, API, performance, and overengineering review found no omission. Independent code review requested one useful WHY comment, re-reviewed it after the focused 19-test/57-assertion recycler run, and signed off. The owner reviewed the final package summary and approved committing.
- **Laravel-facing result:** No Laravel public API, configuration key or structure, documented behavior, or conventional extension pattern changes. Object Pool and Sentry's pool constructor are Hypervel-specific; Hyperf parity is not required. The owner-approved constructor cleanup intentionally removes false Hypervel-specific arguments without a compatibility shim.
- **Assessment:** All three accepted findings are closed. Unrelated pools remain maintainable after one supported custom pool fails, lifecycle controls state their true worker-wide boundary, and constructors advertise and retain only real dependencies. The result adds only one cold per-pool try/catch at the existing maintenance interval and failure-only diagnostics, while deleting false dependencies and retained references. No request-hot-path work, lock, context state, registry, retry, timeout, cache, worker-exit hook, reporting abstraction, compatibility layer, workaround, or speculative machinery remains.

### Expose process stopping through the contract

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `process-01` | Defect | Minor | High | `PendingProcess::start()` returns `Contracts\Process\InvokedProcess`, but that contract omits `stop()` even though both real and fake implementations provide the same method | Add the existing typed `stop()` signature to the contract and prove both implementations can be stopped through the contract type |

- **Contract rationale:** `PendingProcess::start()` returns the contract, so lifecycle-managed callers must be able to stop the returned process without depending on a concrete implementation.
- **Upstream feature trace:** Laravel framework PR `#52959` added `stop()` only to the concrete `InvokedProcess` and `InvokedProcessPool`; it changed no contract, test, fixture, configuration, or documentation file, and its discussion explicitly left process-stop behavior to Symfony coverage. Current Laravel 13.x retains both concrete methods while still omitting `stop()` from its invoked-process contract. Hypervel's contract addition is therefore a deliberate contract-completeness correction under the rule that every conforming invoked process must expose lifecycle behavior returned callers require, not a claim that Laravel added the contract method.
- **Compatibility and performance:** This is an additive contract correction with no runtime path or implementation change. Both existing implementations already satisfy the exact signature.
- **Regression and revalidation:** `ProcessTest` covers real and fake stopping through the contract. The full Process audit repeated the repository-wide implementer search, retained the method, and confirmed that the real and fake invoked processes remain the only implementations.

### Make Process callbacks and pools failure-safe

| ID | Category | Severity | Confidence | Failure and owning boundary | Final decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `process-02` | Defect | Major | High | Exceptions from native output callbacks leave exact child processes running, partial pool startup leaves earlier children unowned, and a failed pool wait abandons live siblings whose external progress may never complete | Stop the exact native child when its callback throws, make pool creation transactional, and stop every sibling after a failed wait while preserving the original failure |
| `process-03` | Defect | Minor | High | The invoked-process contract omits `ensureNotTimedOut()`, and the real implementation forwards nullable `waitUntil()` callbacks to Symfony even though Symfony requires a callable | Complete the contract and make a null `waitUntil()` follow the documented wait-to-completion behavior in both implementations |
| `process-04` | Defect | Minor | High | The fake invoked-process lifecycle reports a PID after stopping, marks a readiness-matched process finished, permanently replaces its output handler, remains live after callback failure, rejects valid non-Closure callables despite its callable API, returns a newline for absent output, and compares signals loosely | Preserve the fake's existing observable scheduling while making PID, readiness, callback assignment/failure, handler restoration, empty output, and signal checks match the real lifecycle |
| `process-05` | Defect | Minor | High | Truthiness checks discard the supported string command, input, and fake output value `"0"`, while environment docblocks exclude Symfony-supported `Stringable` and `false` values | Use null/explicit-empty checks and publish the precise environment type without changing the runtime API |
| `process-06` | Defect | Minor | High | Values passed to `FakeProcessSequence` at construction bypass the normalization used by `push()`, so supported seeded strings and arrays later violate the sequence return contract | Normalize constructor seeds through the existing conversion boundary and narrow every public sequence docblock to the accepted union |
| `process-07` | Defect | Minor | High | Hypervel's current Laravel-ported `InvokedProcess` is missing Laravel's public `Macroable` surface and corresponding test cleanup | Add `Macroable` only to the real public implementation, expose a pure `flushState()`, and register it with framework test cleanup |
| `process-08` | Defect | Minor | High | Running an empty pipe reaches a native return-type failure instead of a useful framework diagnostic | Reject an empty pipe after its builder callback with a descriptive `InvalidArgumentException`; empty process pools remain valid |
| `process-09` | Userland footgun | Minor | High | Test-only fake, recording, and stray-process controls mutate the container-resolved worker singleton, while successfully started asynchronous children remain caller-owned across coroutine teardown | Add concrete test-only worker-lifetime warnings and document that callers must wait for or stop successful asynchronous processes and pools before their owning coroutine exits |

- **Architecture and ownership:** A package-local `Concerns\GuardsProcessOutput` trait owns the one non-trivial repeated callback boundary used by synchronous runs, asynchronous starts, invoked waits, and readiness waits. It captures the exact Symfony process, suppresses callback re-entry while `stop(0)` drains output, stops only after the user callback throws, swallows only cleanup failure, and rethrows the original exception. Its failure bit is closure-local static state: it survives synchronous re-entry within one wrapper while remaining isolated between process operations and avoids both a worker-shared instance property and an analyzer-only state-object allocation. Pool startup validates every pending process before acquiring children, retains unpublished exact handles until all starts succeed, and rolls them back exhaustively on partial failure.
